14 Operating Agents

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why does a configuration that clears its offline evaluation still need evidence gathered while it runs?

An offline evaluation picks its tasks, fixtures, and graders in advance, and live traffic honors none of those choices. Production brings inputs no fixture anticipated, dependencies that slow down under load, and tool results cut short without warning, while every admitted task acts with real authority. When the rate of accepted tasks drops, the count says that something failed but not where, because one trial spreads its model calls, tool executions, and checks across processes and hosts. Finding the cause takes a record of each trial, yet keeping every record costs too much and exposes private data, and sampling records at random discards exactly the rare failures engineers most need. A failure that recurs ten times under a one-percent sample leaves no detailed record about nine times in ten. Even a kept record shows only what happened. It can show that an observation was truncated and that the runtime refused an action, but whether a complete observation would have produced an accepted repair is a question only a controlled rerun from the saved state can answer. Releasing a change raises its own questions. A small canary does not make a regression cheaper to detect, because a monitor needs the same number of tasks at any traffic share; the canary limits only the harm from problems no monitor measures. Rolling back stops new work but does not recall requests already sent or restore files already changed. Operating an agent therefore means deciding in advance what to record, how to test an explanation, how much live work to expose, and what a release must show before it widens, and then writing down what is still unknown. Every exposure the agent carries reaches production at once: the state a failure leaves behind, the horizon over which harm builds before a monitor decides, and the authority whose effects outlast a rollback.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why a diagnostic trace, the durable recovery log, and the accounting record carry different guarantees
  • Analyze a trace’s critical path to attribute elapsed time among overlapping spans
  • Design verdict-aware trace retention that keeps rare failures within storage and privacy budgets
  • Diagnose an agent failure through a controlled intervention, stating what the result does and does not establish
  • Design staged releases that size canary exposure with sequential stopping rules and independent hard tripwires
  • Evaluate an integrated evidence record against a declared conjunctive release gate
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

14.1 From Offline Verdict to Live Operation

The offline comparison of candidate_v2 against baseline_v1 ended without a decision in either direction. The candidate accepted more of the paired tasks, but its paired interval does not exclude a loss larger than the declared tolerance, and a powered follow-up sample is still to be planned. That verdict concerns a configuration on a sampled task population. It says nothing about why a particular trial failed, and nothing about what the configuration does once it acts on live work, where inputs are not fixtures, dependencies are shared, and every admitted task exercises real authority.

↩︎ The paired comparison that leaves candidate_v2 inconclusive against its noninferiority margin is established in Paired outcome comparison.

Operating a configuration carries every exposure at once: the state a failure leaves, the horizon before a monitor decides, and the authority a rollback cannot recall.

The repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) supplies this chapter’s case. One trial of candidate_v2 on the connection-pool repair fails after its search tool returns a silently truncated result, and the next model response proposes rewriting a file outside the permitted repair scope, which the runtime refuses before dispatch. Aggregate counts record the failure; they cannot say whether the model, the observation adapter, a slow dependency, or the runtime is responsible, or whether repairing one of them would change the outcome. Answering those questions takes evidence the offline harness never collected, gathered while the system runs and kept long enough to inspect.

Four kinds of operational evidence fill that gap, and each has its own limits. A trace binds every model call, tool execution, and check to its trial, so that a delay or a failure can be attributed along the trajectory’s critical path (section 14.2). Retention policy decides which traces survive storage and privacy budgets, and the accounting record keeps the evaluation denominator complete when diagnostic detail is sampled (section 14.3). An incident postmortem turns an observed mismatch into a hypothesis and tests it by intervening on a saved prefix of the recorded trajectory, since no amount of rereading historical telemetry can answer a counterfactual (section 14.4). Staged release then exposes a revised configuration to live work only as fast as sequential evidence and hard tripwires allow, because a rollback stops new admission but settles nothing already done (section 14.5). The chapter closes by joining these records with the offline comparison into one decision record and a declared release rule (section 14.6), beginning with the trace that explains where the truncated-observation trial spent its time.

14.2 Distributed Trajectory Tracing

When a trial of candidate_v2 on the connection-pool repair fails or stalls, aggregate summary metrics record only the wall-clock total and the final verdict, blending remote inference stalls, blocked test containers, and malformed or truncated tool outputs into one undifferentiated number. A trace separates those possibilities by binding every interaction back to the specific evaluation trial, candidate revision, and runtime operation, replacing disjointed service logs with an end-to-end causal record.

Distributed tracing tracks units of work across process and network boundaries, an operational model established at production scale by Dapper (Sigelman et al. 2010). At the core of this model, a span represents a single contiguous unit of execution defined by a unique identifier, explicit start and end timestamps, structured attributes, and optional timed events. Hierarchical parent-child relationships capture synchronous nesting, while trace links correlate decoupled asynchronous interactions that fall outside a single call tree, following the standard OpenTelemetry model (OpenTelemetry Authors 2026). Within an agentic trajectory, a large language model (LLM) generation step, a mediated tool execution in a sandbox, and subsequent result validation each form distinct spans. Omitting instrumentation at any of these boundaries leaves unmonitored voids, obscuring whether an unexpected delay originated in model inference, queueing overhead, or environment setup.

OpenTelemetry Authors. 2026. Traces. OpenTelemetry documentation.

14.2.1 Diagnostic capture and recovery history

During the connection-pool repair, every model invocation and sandbox tool call emits both a durable log record and a diagnostic trace span, often carrying the same operation identifier. Treating those two streams as interchangeable is a dangerous operational shortcut. If an execution engine relies on its diagnostic tracing pipeline to reconstruct state after a worker crash, a single load-shedding event that drops spans or truncates large tool payloads will corrupt recovery. Separating the recovery path from the observability path ensures that telemetry backpressure never compromises execution durability.

Systems Perspective 14.1: Recovery history and diagnostic traces
The durable trajectory log preserves the authoritative, acknowledged state transitions and input artifacts required to reconstruct execution state after a failure. A diagnostic telemetry stream that happens to share identical span identifiers cannot replace this log. Diagnostic traces prioritize performance analysis, bottleneck detection, and interactive debugging; under high load or network partitions, tracing pipelines legitimately sample traces, truncate large payloads, or drop spans. The same runtime event can emit records to both pipelines, but their guarantees differ completely. An observability agent might discard an optional span to protect telemetry egress, while the durable recovery engine must guarantee that operation identifiers, resource reservations, and partial results persist to durable storage.

↩︎ The durable trajectory log, from which recovery reconstructs execution state, is established in The log as recorded evidence.

Systems engineers must maintain this distinction when tracking asynchronous operations such as the test run on ws_r18 that recovery left unresolved before evaluation began. When an HTTP client monitoring that sandbox times out and terminates its diagnostic span, the underlying test does not vanish. The workload continues running inside the container until the sandbox emits an authenticated callback with the test results, which the operation table records against the original operation while the trajectory stays paused. The distributed trace reflects that split execution without rerunning the test or resuming the model prematurely. It correlates the late-arriving callback with the original dispatch attempt through a trace link, which preserves both the client-side timeout and the true server-side execution lifetime.

Captured content demands an equally strict boundary. An evaluation harness must distinguish between what an agent proposed, what intermediate middleware modified, and what the model actually received. The delivered-input manifest records the exact byte sequence presented to the model context window, which frequently diverges from raw tool output due to prompt templating, token budget truncation, or context compression. Cryptographic hashes verify payload integrity against historical records, but a hash digest cannot reconstruct payload bytes that were omitted or pruned from storage. Exact deterministic replay and forensic debugging therefore require preserving delivered-input manifests and raw observations in content-addressed storage alongside the trace metadata.

14.2.2 Trace evidence fields

Preserving input manifests and span records helps little if each service emits unstandardized telemetry tags that omit which trial, candidate revision, transport attempt, or truncation policy produced an observation. An evaluation harness instead requires a disciplined, minimal schema that links experimental parameters directly to runtime and network observations. Table 1 organizes the essential fields into five functional categories. The attribute names may follow a standard such as the OpenTelemetry semantic conventions; the five groups are the minimal information required to diagnose agent behavior.

This schema carries the task, trial, configuration, and artifact distinctions of the acceptance record into each observed interaction, while constraining provider provenance to what the remote interface exposes. In evaluation tracing, span status must remain decoupled from the final task verdict. An operating system process running a test suite may exit with code 0, prompting the tracing layer to mark the span successful, even though the test output reveals that the candidate failed critical assertions. Conversely, a client span might terminate with a timeout error while fetching auxiliary documentation, yet the candidate agent may still synthesize a valid patch from cached context. The authoritative task acceptance decision relies on independent verification suites and often arrives long after the agent’s execution spans have closed.

Propagating context across architectural boundaries such as an API gateway, an asynchronous queue, or an isolated worker sandbox demands standardized metadata carriers. The W3C Trace Context specification (W3C Distributed Tracing Working Group 2021) provides standard HTTP and messaging headers to propagate correlation identifiers across distributed components. Trace identifiers, however, establish correlation, not authorization. A worker sandbox receiving a trace identifier cannot treat it as an admission token, nor can an agent bypass security policies by injecting arbitrary parent headers. Admission control, credential validation, and payload sanitization remain the exclusive responsibility of the runtime host. Furthermore, trace headers must never carry sensitive prompt tokens, credentials, or proprietary task data across untrusted network boundaries. When black-box tools or legacy binaries cannot emit internal spans, the orchestrator must record host-observed boundaries, explicitly tagging the span as an external dispatch-to-response measurement rather than an internal execution trace.

W3C Distributed Tracing Working Group. 2021. Trace Context: W3C Recommendation 23 November 2021. World Wide Web Consortium.

14.2.3 Span overlap and latency attribution

Summing span durations to obtain total agent execution time is a common error in telemetry analysis. Because a parent span encloses the lifetimes of its children, adding parent and child durations double-counts work. Furthermore, when an agent orchestrator fires multiple tool calls concurrently, for example a static analysis linter alongside a targeted test suite, their child spans overlap on the timeline. Execution order stems strictly from completion dependencies, never from parent-child hierarchy alone. Comparing durations measured on a single monotonic clock is straightforward, but correlating timestamps across distributed worker hosts requires an explicit alignment model.1 Without synchronized time sources or clock-offset modeling, apparent span overlap between distinct machines can reflect nothing more than physical clock skew. Instrumentation boundaries introduce similar attribution challenges. Client-observed model latency encompasses TCP connection handshakes, TLS negotiation, queueing delay at the inference gateway, and network transfer time, so time to first token is never an isolated measurement of model prefill compute.

1 Happened-Before Order in a Trace: The partial order that Harness regression replay used to match replayed requests applies to spans as well. Parent and link relations recorded at dispatch carry causal order between spans on different hosts; cross-host timestamps without an alignment model do not, so two spans that appear to overlap may be ordered, and two that appear ordered may be concurrent.

2 Critical Path Method: Critical-path planning was formalized in 1959 for scheduling large engineering projects, computing for each activity the float by which it can slip without delaying completion (Kelley and Walker 1959). The notebook measures a related quantity, the duration gap between the critical branch and the next-longest parallel path.

Kelley, James E., and Morgan R. Walker. 1959. “Critical-Path Planning and Scheduling.” Papers Presented at the December 1-3, 1959, Eastern Joint IRE-AIEE-ACM Computer Conference, 160–73. https://doi.org/10.1145/1460299.1460318.

To quantify where latency optimizations yield true wall-clock savings, systems engineers measure the critical path through the trajectory’s dependency graph, the longest chain of dependent operations recorded in the trace.2 Sequential operations add their durations directly, whereas concurrent branches that join at a common synchronization barrier contribute only the duration of the slowest branch. Shortening a branch off the critical path leaves elapsed time unchanged, and shortening one on it helps only until the next-longest branch takes its place (notebook 14.1).

↪ An acceleration is priced by the time it removes from this critical path, a trade-off developed in Critical-Path Latency.

The hypothetical intervals used in notebook 14.1 share a single host clock. Figure 1 places them on a common axis so that the parallel checks, their join, and the unaccounted interval remain visible together.

The join prevents a faster lint check from shortening this segment while tests remain the longer branch. The later synthesis start also leaves a gap that these spans do not explain. Neither nesting nor a successful callback supplies the separate evidence required to accept the task.

Napkin Math 14.1: Observed time and the critical path
Problem: How much elapsed time do the recorded spans explain, and how much would shortening one check reduce the segment if the other durations and dependencies stayed unchanged?

Assumptions: Table 2 gives observations from a separate hypothetical timing trial that inspects ws_r19. The model first proposes checks, the runtime admits two independent read-only checks, and synthesis waits for both. All intervals use one host clock, no hidden dependency delays either check’s start, and admission takes no separate time. The enclosing segment runs from retrieval dispatch through the final response.

Math: Adding all leaf durations yields 13.2 s, an overestimate that double-counts concurrent execution during the validation phase. Following the path through retrieval, planning, the longer check (targeted tests), and synthesis, which counts the overlapping checks once, explains 11.2 s. The remaining 0.5 s occurs between the completion of the slowest check and the dispatch of the final synthesis call. Because the trace lacks instrumentation for intermediate runtime bookkeeping, prompt serialization, or context collation, this duration remains unattributed. An unmeasured interval highlights a boundary where instrumentation must be refined rather than evidence of worker idle time.

Intervention: Suppose an optimization accelerates the targeted test suite from 6.0 s down to 1 s while all other components remain unchanged. Because the static lint check requires 2.0 s, lint replaces the test suite as the bottleneck on the parallel branch. Recomputing the critical path yields 7.7 s for the total segment, achieving an overall elapsed reduction of 4 s.

Systems insight: A performance optimization on a parallel execution branch yields wall-clock returns only up to the duration gap to the next-longest concurrent path. Accelerating the test suite by 5 s yields only 4 s of end-to-end savings because static linting immediately becomes the new critical-path limiter. Systems architects must recompute the critical path across the entire dependency graph before estimating the value of an optimization. Furthermore, theoretical speedups must always be verified empirically in controlled trials; real-world optimizations can shift resource contention, alter cache locality, change garbage collection pressure, or elicit different token generation lengths from the model.

Checkpoint 14.1: Interpret the trace's evidence

Distributed trajectory tracing provides the causal fabric needed to diagnose why an agent succeeded, failed, or stalled. However, capturing detailed telemetry incurs concrete engineering costs in CPU overhead, network bandwidth, and persistent storage. In high-throughput evaluation harnesses, preserving every token, tool payload, and internal span across thousands of benchmark trials is rarely feasible. Defining what to measure is only the first half of the observability problem; the second half is establishing capture and retention policies that preserve critical failure evidence without overwhelming the telemetry infrastructure.

14.3 Trace Retention and Privacy

If a delayed regression suite for the trial that produced ws_r19 fails hours after execution concludes, that trial suddenly becomes diagnostically indispensable. By that time, however, its runtime process has terminated and its model and tool spans have closed. The forensic value of the run depends entirely on what the telemetry system chose to keep. Trace retention therefore requires integration with final task verdicts and their arrival timelines, rather than relying purely on process exit codes or span completion markers. Because retaining exhaustive execution histories rapidly escalates storage expenditures and expands sensitive data exposure, systems designers must determine which evidence each evaluation goal demands, and which diagnostic guarantees remain possible when capture budgets are constrained.

14.3.1 Accounting records and head sampling

If a telemetry pipeline samples trial registrations at the same rate as diagnostic span bodies, any trial that times out, crashes a worker, or drops its spans disappears from the evaluation denominator, biasing pass rates toward fast, easy runs. Beyond the recovery history and diagnostic traces separated in perspective 14.1, evaluation requires a third artifact: the accounting record. This record catalogs every admitted trial, its exact environment configuration, evaluation criteria, current verdict status, measured resource expenditures, and missing telemetry markers. While these three records can reside on shared physical storage, sampling policies must apply exclusively to diagnostic detail. Telemetry systems must never sample recovery history or the evaluation denominator.

Diagnostic detail also expands substantially faster than the underlying trajectory when spans mirror raw message history. If each model-call span replicates the entire conversation context sent to the provider, diagnostic trace volume inherits the quadratic resend pattern formalized in equation 2.9. Telemetry systems avoid this runaway expansion by referencing immutable conversation prefixes via cryptographic content digests and storing each new tool observation exactly once. This content-addressable deduplication keeps storage growth linear in the number of turns, provided the underlying blob store retains the referenced content for the lifetime of the trace.

Head sampling decides whether to retain detailed diagnostic traces at the initial entry point of execution. Drawing a random sample with recorded inclusion probabilities allows engineers to compute unbiased population estimates for metrics such as mean latency or aggregate token consumption. However, uniform head sampling struggles with rare failure modes, as an intermittent defect can easily slip through without leaving a single retained trace.

Because head sampling decides retention before execution outcomes are known, each occurrence of a failure mode is retained independently with sampling probability \(\HeadSamplingProbability\). Consequently, a failure mode that occurs \(\FailureModeOccurrenceCount\) times leaves no detailed diagnostic trace with probability \((1-\HeadSamplingProbability)^{\FailureModeOccurrenceCount}\). At \(\HeadSamplingProbability =\) 10 percent, a failure mode that occurs 5 times carries roughly a 59 percent probability of leaving zero detailed examples in storage. Only when that mode recurs 50 times does the probability of a complete miss fall to 0.5 percent. By its mathematical nature, uniform sampling is least likely to preserve the rare, anomalous executions that engineers most urgently need to inspect.

To capture these elusive events without retaining every routine execution, tail sampling defers the retention decision until operational evidence accumulates downstream, such as a failed acceptance assertion, an unhandled tool exception, an unexpected human intervention, or an anomalous latency spike. Tail selection deliberately concentrates diagnostic storage on high-value, problematic runs, producing a trace collection that is intentionally unrepresentative of normal production traffic. Engineers must therefore compute population failure rates from the complete accounting record rather than from the tail-sampled trace collection. Whenever teams derive statistical estimates from sampled records, the telemetry infrastructure must record the exact inclusion probabilities alongside the active selection policy version.3 Diagnostic workflows also require a baseline for comparison. The pipeline must retain a known random sample of ordinary, accepted trials as a control group, because an isolated failure trace cannot reveal which intermediate steps were anomalous and which were routine.

3 Uniform Trace Sampling: The first production version of one large-scale tracing system sampled uniformly, averaging one traced request per 1,024 candidates, which sufficed for high-traffic services because events of interest recurred often enough to be captured; lower-traffic workloads could miss important events at that rate (Sigelman et al. 2010). Its later adaptive scheme targeted a rate of sampled traces per unit time and recorded the sampling probability with each trace, the inclusion-probability bookkeeping that population estimates require.

Sigelman, Benjamin H, Luiz André Barroso, Mike Burrows, Pat Stephenson, Manoj Plakal, Donald Beaver, Saul Jaspan, and Chandan Shanbhag. 2010. Dapper, a Large-Scale Distributed Systems Tracing Infrastructure. Dapper-2010-1. Google.

14.3.2 Tail sampling and verdict windows

If a tail-sampling buffer evicts trace bodies before a slow regression suite finishes grading ws_r19, the accounting record logs the late failure while the spans needed to explain it are already gone. Because tail selection spans multiple operational stages, an execution trace is held provisionally in a staging buffer while acceptance checks run, receiving its final retention classification only when conclusive evaluation evidence arrives within a declared verdict window. When the staging buffer expires before an asynchronous failure is reported, the accounting record must explicitly flag the missing diagnostic detail so audit integrity holds. Table 3 outlines this tiered retention policy, establishing a strict precedence order so that overlapping classification rules do not count or persist a single trace multiple times.

Eviction priority: When ring buffers experience memory pressure, eviction policies must shed routine successes and unclassified traces before dropping runs flagged for policy violations or failed assertions. While differential eviction priority substantially improves diagnostic survival rates, extreme burst traffic can still cause trace loss.

Physical buffer limits inevitably govern this architecture. Buffer managers must enforce four concurrent bounds: active trace count, aggregate byte volume, individual trace size limits, and maximum staging age. Sizing from averages, such as mean concurrent traces multiplied by mean trace size, is a frequent engineering trap, because cascading failures typically produce bursts of unusually long, tool-heavy trajectories that quickly exhaust buffer memory. Telemetry systems must expose real-time metrics for head-dropped records, buffer evictions, and span truncations broken down by cohort and selection class. Without this visibility, correlated loss during incidents will silently purge the exact failure traces engineers need to diagnose system outages. Furthermore, while deferred tail selection curtails long-term downstream storage, it does not reduce the network ingress and memory needed to buffer traces prior to selection, and strict privacy restrictions apply to these staging buffers as well. The accounting record therefore sits outside the buffer (figure 2). Every admitted trial enters it directly, together with every recorded drop, eviction, and truncation, so the denominator survives whatever the buffer loses.

Napkin Math 14.2: What verdict-aware selection saves
Problem: How much downstream diagnostic storage do uniform head sampling and verdict-aware tail selection use for the same hypothetical workload?

Assumptions: Each hour brings 10,000 trials with equal 1.4 MB permitted trace bodies. At the selection boundary, 400 have failed criteria, 100 remain pending or unknown, and 9,500 are ordinary accepted trials. These classes are disjoint, repeat for 24 hours, and all survive collection. Head selection samples each trial with probability 5 percent. Tail selection retains the failed and unresolved classes and independently samples 2 percent of ordinary accepted trials.

Math: Keeping every body requires 336 GB over the stated period. Head sampling retains an expected 500 traces per hour, or 16.8 GB over the stated period. It includes an expected 20 failed and 5 unresolved trials per hour, not guaranteed counts. Tail selection retains 400 failed, 100 unresolved, and an expected 190 ordinary accepted trials: 690 traces per hour and 23.184 GB over the stated period.

Result: Full retention uses about 14.5× the downstream bytes of the tail policy. Tail selection consumes more capacity than uniform head sampling in this scenario, but it guarantees diagnostic visibility for every failed and unresolved execution rather than retaining only an unpredictable handful of anomalous runs.

Systems insight: Retained volume is governed by two terms. The first is an inelastic storage floor determined by the failure-plus-unresolved rate (16.8 GB per day in this workload). The second is an adjustable overhead determined by the control sampling fraction on routine successes, where each percentage point of control sampling adds 3.2 GB per day. Capacity planning must size the baseline floor to accommodate elevated failure rates during service disruptions while dialing the control rate to fit available long-term storage budgets. These calculations reflect decimal gigabytes for raw trace payloads; indexing metadata, replication factors, accounting records, and upstream ingestion buffers impose additional overhead.

14.3.3 Disclosure minimization

Dumping raw model prompts and unredacted tool responses directly into a shared tracing backend turns the observability pipeline into a secondary data leak, spreading customer records, database keys, and proprietary source snippets across search indexes and crash logs where retroactive scrubbing cannot reach them. Diagnostic investigations rarely require wholesale ingestion of raw prompts and complete tool outputs; structured metadata (typed return codes, schema versions, sub-span latencies, policy decisions, and hashed artifact pointers) often provides complete debugging context without exposing sensitive user data. Even when credentials and API tokens are cleanly scrubbed, tool arguments and responses frequently contain private records or personal identifiers. Authorization policies must therefore govern specific data attributes and restrict access by investigator role, rather than granting blanket read access to an entire telemetry tier. Data minimization belongs at the trusted capture boundary inside the execution runtime, before unscrubbed buffers enter network queues, ingestion daemons, or alerting systems.

Automated redaction mitigates disclosure risks, but systems designers must treat every scrubbing mechanism as inherently fallible. Regular expressions miss non-standard or obfuscated secret patterns. Shannon entropy detectors flag UUIDs and cryptographic nonces while missing predictable credentials. Named-entity recognition models err, skipping private names or stripping valid operational syntax required to diagnose a tool error. Because heuristic sanitizers cannot guarantee zero disclosure across arbitrary payloads, architectural schema design must prioritize structural field exclusion over text redaction, so that unnecessary fields are never captured in the first place. For retained fields that undergo transformation, pipelines must validate sanitization rules against curated benchmark suites containing real-world sensitive patterns. When downstream diagnosis requires correlating multiple actions by the same entity without revealing real-world identities, telemetry systems should replace identifiers with keyed cryptographic pseudonyms. Generating these pseudonyms via a hash-based message authentication code (HMAC) requires a securely managed secret key rather than a public salt (Krawczyk et al. 1997, sec. 2). However, engineers must recognize that persistent pseudonyms preserve relational linkability across traces. Regular key rotation limits longitudinal correlation across evaluation epochs, at the cost of diagnoses that must follow one entity across several weeks. Pseudonymization is not complete anonymization, nor does it justify indefinite trace retention.

Krawczyk, Hugo, Mihir Bellare, and Ran Canetti. 1997. HMAC: Keyed-Hashing for Message Authentication. RFC 2104. Internet Engineering Task Force. https://doi.org/10.17487/RFC2104.

14.3.4 Replay fidelity after redaction

Stripping a sensitive database column from a tool response can conceal the exact formatting defect that triggered a model’s parsing error, and replacing an address string with a placeholder or pseudonym can shift sub-word token boundaries and steer the model down an entirely different decoding path during re-execution. Because scrubbing or masking input tokens alters the precise byte sequences that drove agent behavior, sanitization creates an inherent tension between privacy enforcement and replay fidelity: a redacted trace cannot serve as a bit-exact substitute for the environment interactions that actually occurred. When forensic investigations require exact replay and compliance policies permit it, systems must isolate raw execution payloads in a dedicated, access-restricted artifact repository, completely separate from general diagnostic telemetry. The diagnostic trace then stores only cryptographic hashes pointing to these secured blobs, alongside metadata recording the exact transformation pipeline used to generate the sanitized view. When raw payloads expire under data-retention schedules or cannot be kept due to privacy constraints, teams must formally narrow the scope of their diagnostic claims. Replaying an agent against synthetic mock fixtures validates whether the model handles a class of problems in a fresh run, but it does not prove what occurred during the original production execution.

Checkpoint 14.2: Retained evidence and supported conclusions

Storage budgets determine which runtime observations survive, while privacy policies govern which preserved artifacts an investigator may legally inspect. Every postmortem analysis and regression benchmark operates within the boundary those two constraints set.

14.4 Incident Postmortems

When verdict-aware retention preserves the failed connection-pool repair trial of candidate_v2 (section 14.1), the retained trace shows the search response ending in the middle of a source excerpt with no truncation notice, even though the bounded-observation contract requires one, followed by a model response that proposes replacing a file outside the permitted repair scope. The runtime rejects that proposal before dispatch. While the execution trace pinpoints where the observation was cut short and where the runtime blocked the illegal operation, it cannot reveal whether a complete observation would have produced an accepted repair. An incident postmortem4 connects such telemetry to a testable explanation and a scoped corrective change.

4 Blameless Postmortem: Site reliability engineering conducts blameless postmortems to identify contributing causes without indicting any individual or team (Beyer et al. 2016, ch. 15). Agent incidents extend this stance from people to components: a model, an observation adapter, and an admission gate can each contribute, and naming one culprit early hides the conditions the corrective change must address.

Beyer, Betsy, Chris Jones, Jennifer Petoff, and Niall Richard Murphy. 2016. Site Reliability Engineering: How Google Runs Production Systems. O’Reilly Media.

↩︎ The bounded-observation contract, under which an adapter that cuts a result must say so in a typed truncation notice, is established in Truncation notices and inspection handles.

Rigorous postmortems keep these initial findings strictly separated. Delivering an excerpt without truncation metadata demonstrates an interface defect. Emitting an out-of-scope file replacement reveals how the foundation model behaved when starved of context. Intercepting that illegal write confirms that the admission gate functioned as designed. Because failures in agentic systems typically stem from compounding interactions across subsystems, prematurely pinning the fault on a single component obscures the precise conditions that engineering fixes must resolve.

14.4.1 Reconstruction and replay

Rereading a static trace cannot show whether candidate_v2 would have repaired the connection pool given an untruncated search result. Re-running a modified agent against recorded tool outputs without request matching can also pair new model calls with stale environment replies. The three uses of a recorded trajectory from durable execution become the postmortem’s three instruments, each answering a distinct diagnostic question:

  • Time-travel replay folds recorded decisions from a compatible execution checkpoint to recover the exact system state visible to the agent at the moment of failure.
  • Harness regression replay executes deterministic component code against captured inputs while disabling live external side effects.
  • A counterfactual branch forks execution from a saved prefix, altering a specific variable or interface contract and collecting fresh observations along the divergent path.

↩︎ The three uses of a recorded trajectory, and the limits of what each one shows, are established in Recorded Trajectory Replay.

Time-travel replay only inspects the past, and no replay mode can synthesize an unrecorded historical outcome. Demonstrating that a revised routine now passes its tests does not change what the original trial delivered, observed, or did. Furthermore, the fidelity of time-travel replay remains strictly bounded by the capture contract defined in perspective 14.1. Examining the delivered context reveals the truncated excerpt presented to the model. Reviewing separately preserved raw stdout reveals what the adapter dropped. Inspecting redacted logs reveals only execution metadata and high-level status.

Harness regression replay is especially effective for isolating data transformations without incurring expensive model invocations. Supplying captured tool responses to legacy and revised observation adapters, with live effect paths disabled, deterministically verifies that the legacy adapter omitted a required partial-result marker while the revised adapter correctly supplies it. However, this offline harness test cannot predict how a model will respond to the revised observation envelope. Answering that question requires a live experiment. Any alteration to prompt text, sampling temperature, model configuration, or tool observation formats represents an intervention rather than a passive replay. Recorded downstream responses remain reusable only under the matching-request rule of Hermetic Evaluation Gyms; matching solely on tool names risks fabricating artificial successes by pairing new model actions with obsolete environment responses.

14.4.2 Fault attribution

Blaming the model for proposing an out-of-scope file rewrite, or trusting the model’s own retrospective explanation of why it failed, confuses a downstream symptom with the upstream truncation defect that triggered it. In the connection-pool incident, comparing the search tool’s raw output against the delivered envelope confirms that the observation adapter silently truncated the text. That finding yields an engineering hypothesis, namely that omitting the completeness signal misled the model into generating inappropriate repair proposals. Validating this hypothesis demands empirical outcomes measured after an intervention rather than repeated scrutiny of historical telemetry. Table 4 distinguishes useful evidence from claims that telemetry cannot support alone; its rows represent overlapping investigation paths rather than mutually exclusive diagnoses.

System diagnostics must account for this ambiguity at every layer, because neither low-level system signals nor high-level model outputs are self-explanatory. An operating-system signal that terminates a container cannot distinguish an out-of-memory eviction from an administrative kill. Likewise, a schema-valid model response can still rest on unverified assumptions, and an agent’s retrospective self-explanation remains a generative hypothesis rather than empirical evidence.

14.4.3 Controlled diagnostic experiments

If an engineer ships an omission marker and pagination together while external service latency drifts between runs, a higher pass rate cannot distinguish whether the marker, the pagination, or the quieter infrastructure fixed the failure. To move from suspicion to actionable diagnosis, a controlled ablation protocol defines five elements before execution: the baseline checkpoint boundary, the specific architectural intervention, all frozen system configurations, allowable sources of stochastic variation, and uniform acceptance criteria. Resetting repository files while allowing external service behaviors to drift introduces confounding variables; interleaving experimental trials across branches helps decouple the intervention effect from infrastructure drift. Disentangling bundled changes such as a partial-result marker and pagination reads requires factorial conditions that vary each mechanism independently. The NIST discussion of one-factor experiments demonstrates why varying a single factor at a time fails to expose cross-component interactions (Guthrie 2020, sec. 5.2.1.2).

Guthrie, William F. 2020. NIST/SEMATECH e-Handbook of Statistical Methods (NIST Handbook 151). National Institute of Standards and Technology. https://doi.org/10.18434/M32189.

Example 14.1: A controlled test of observation completeness
Problem: A diagnostic fixture reproduces a silently truncated search result during the connection-pool repair. What do the following hypothetical outcomes support about a proposed interface change?

Scenario: The experiment restores a private copy of a compatible saved prefix of pool_repair_01 for every continuation, under three branches: the original interface, an added warning, and a revised paginated interface. Each run maintains its own execution lineage and identity, meaning these continuation trials offer evidence about counterfactual behavior without altering the recorded history of the trials that produced ws_r18 or ws_r19. All conditions share the model configuration, sampling settings, starting candidate, permitted operations, budget, and checks, and each receives 20 fresh stochastic continuations. Acceptance uses the repair’s criteria and rejects any run that proposes the out-of-scope rewrite, which the unchanged admission gate blocks before dispatch, and every run reaches a known assessment.

The original-interface condition deliberately retains the adapter defect, delivering the truncated excerpt without an omission notice, and yields 1 accepted repair alongside 19 rejected runs that propose the forbidden rewrite. By contrast, a warning-only condition adds an instruction that search results may be incomplete without providing a continuation mechanism, producing 8 accepted repairs and 12 budget-limited runs that never reach an accepted repair, though none proposes the forbidden rewrite. Finally, the revised-interface condition supplies typed partial-result metadata together with a supported way to retrieve remaining results, achieving 20 accepted repairs with zero nonaccepted runs and no forbidden rewrite proposals. Across all three conditions, the admission gate prevents any unauthorized write from dispatching.

Diagnosis: Suppressing an illegal write proposal is not the same as successfully repairing the connection pool. While adding a static warning discourages the model from proposing out-of-scope file modifications, several continuations exhaust their execution budgets without reaching an acceptable fix. Conversely, the paginated interface achieves a complete success rate across these trials, justifying broader evaluation of that interface package. However, this diagnostic outcome does not prove that the metadata marker alone drove the turnaround, nor does it imply that model behavior was blameless in the original incident.

These stochastic continuations characterize performance strictly conditional on this specific saved prefix and fixture environment. Even achieving 20 accepted runs out of 20 trials still leaves an upper bound of 16 percent on the failure rate under the Wilson score interval of Confidence intervals for acceptance. Furthermore, these diagnostic ablation runs must remain segregated from benchmark task-set evaluations; pooling them into release metrics would artificially inflate sample size and distort generalization estimates.

Systems insight: The observation adapter has an explicit, reproducible defect, and the counterfactual trials demonstrate that repairing it substantially improves continuation outcomes. When designing diagnostic experiments, engineers should preserve admission controls across all conditions, distinguish between safety enforcement (blocking forbidden actions) and functional utility (completing the task), and validate patches against diverse held-out tasks before declaring release readiness.

Because the revised interface restores the bounded-observation contract that the original adapter violated, pagination mechanisms must maintain snapshot consistency against an immutable source revision. Exposing a continuation token for content that the server cannot subsequently serve would substitute one observation defect for another.

14.4.4 Scoped regression tests

Testing an observation-adapter fix only through full end-to-end agent runs wastes model tokens on a deterministic serialization rule, whereas running only the adapter unit test never verifies that the repaired agent actually completes the connection-pool fix. A corrective patch therefore requires verification at two distinct architectural levels: the localized boundary where the defect occurred and the end-to-end task pipeline whose reliability it aims to restore.

At the boundary level, unit tests must remain entirely deterministic and decoupled from stochastic model calls. Observation-adapter test suites should systematically inject complete, truncated, malformed, and empty payloads, verifying that serialized envelopes correctly populate status fields and omission metadata. In parallel, admission-gate suites must directly submit synthesized out-of-scope file modifications to confirm that the policy engine blocks illegal writes prior to dispatch. Neither verification step requires invoking an LLM.

At the system level, regression tests execute the modified agent within an isolated fixture and evaluate the resulting candidate against the full acceptance suite. Engineers should never treat a bare runtime status of COMPLETED as an indicator of correctness, nor should they mistake a single passing test run for a guarantee of operational stability. Table 5 organizes the essential artifacts of an investigation, separating confirmed defects from ongoing statistical uncertainty.

Checkpoint 14.3: Diagnosis and regression scope

Use the repair experiment to distinguish a localized defect from a release claim.

An incident postmortem fulfills its engineering objective when it documents an established interface defect, provides a reproducible regression fixture, and verifies a targeted fix. However, resolving a single localized failure does not guarantee general reliability across diverse production workloads. Staged release evaluation determines which additional tasks, edge cases, and runtime conditions the candidate system must navigate while teams gather the next tier of empirical evidence.

14.5 Staged Releases

Although the revised observation interface produced accepted repairs on the isolated fixture in section 14.4, promoting that revision straight to full production traffic would expose live repositories and external services to unmeasured failure modes. That fixture result supports only testing the change on a broader task set; production authority is a separate grant. A release decision connects the change to its evidence and to the consequences of being wrong, because when an agent regression triggers a rollback, stopping new traffic does not undo tool calls, state mutations, or cloud allocations that remain in flight.

Staging separates evidence available before live execution from evidence that requires it. Offline trials compare configurations on declared fixtures. Shadow execution tests a candidate against authorized copies of current inputs without assigning it the live task. After that, a canary5 assigns a bounded portion of eligible live tasks to the candidate under an explicit release policy. The canary is the ramped online controlled experiment of web services, applied to agent configurations (Kohavi et al. 2009). Each stage answers its own question, and causal and statistical release evidence (principle 12) supports a scoped decision at each step. Failure at any stage routes to the same enforced admission hold (figure 3), which stops new candidate work while recovery settles the work already in flight. The subsections that follow develop the criterion each stage applies.

5 Canary Release: The name borrows from coal miners, who carried canaries underground as an early warning of toxic gas. The analogy holds in one respect and misleads in another: a canary detects a hazard by meeting it first, so a small allocation warns early but does not protect the tasks it exposes.

14.5.1 Offline comparison and shadow evidence

Replaying live user requests through a candidate in shadow mode before it clears its paired offline comparison, or mirroring inputs without isolating side effects and checking recipient permissions, can leak private data to new endpoints or mutate production records twice. The offline decision therefore comes first, using the paired comparison of Paired outcome comparison with the same criteria, sampling unit, budgets, and predeclared margin; comparing separate Wilson lower bounds does not answer it. In the worked case the lower difference bound misses the tolerance, so the candidate stays offline, and favorable traces leave the unmet gate unmet.

For a configuration that clears the offline gate, shadow execution copies each input into a separate execution with its own identity and allowance, while the baseline still serves the original task. Privacy and authorization checks must permit the copied input, its destinations, and the shadow tool operations, since discarding the output does not license sending private data to a new service. The shadow environment also bounds the answerable questions. An isolated stateful replica supports checks of the resulting artifact, a recorded-response fixture supports a narrower comparison under the matching-request rule, and a synthetic success reply to an unperformed mutation establishes nothing about production. Table 6 distinguishes the live task from the additional experiment.

Read access still has effects. Queries can acquire locks, consume quotas, trigger logging, or disclose arguments, so the shadow design names the services it contacts, relying on sandbox isolation and tool mediation.

Tool selection, token use, and latency differences can expose a changed operating pattern before live task assignment. For tool selection, the Jensen–Shannon divergence (Lin 1991) between the baseline and candidate distributions of tool calls, \(\BaselineToolCallDistribution\) and \(\CandidateToolCallDistribution\), summarizes the change in one number. It is built from the Kullback–Leibler divergence \(\KlDivergence(\BaselineToolCallDistribution\,\|\,\CandidateToolCallDistribution)=\sum_\ToolCallCategory \BaselineToolCallDistribution(\ToolCallCategory)\ln\bigl(\BaselineToolCallDistribution(\ToolCallCategory)/\CandidateToolCallDistribution(\ToolCallCategory)\bigr)\), the expected log-ratio between two distributions over tool calls \(\ToolCallCategory\), which is zero only when they match:

Lin, Jianhua. 1991. “Divergence Measures Based on the Shannon Entropy.” IEEE Transactions on Information Theory 37 (1): 145–51. https://doi.org/10.1109/18.61115.

\[\JensenShannonDivergence(\BaselineToolCallDistribution\,\|\,\CandidateToolCallDistribution)=\tfrac12 \KlDivergence(\BaselineToolCallDistribution\,\|\,\JensenShannonMixtureDistribution)+\tfrac12 \KlDivergence(\CandidateToolCallDistribution\,\|\,\JensenShannonMixtureDistribution),\qquad \JensenShannonMixtureDistribution=\tfrac12(\BaselineToolCallDistribution+\CandidateToolCallDistribution). \tag{1}\]

Unlike \(\KlDivergence(\BaselineToolCallDistribution\,\|\,\CandidateToolCallDistribution)\), which divides by zero whenever one configuration calls a tool the other never uses, \(\JensenShannonDivergence\) compares each distribution against their average \(\JensenShannonMixtureDistribution\). Every tool used by either configuration has a mixture probability of at least half its single-configuration share, so the probability ratio against \(\JensenShannonMixtureDistribution(\ToolCallCategory)\) never exceeds \(2\) and its natural logarithm never exceeds \(\ln 2\). That bound makes \(\JensenShannonDivergence\) symmetric and confines it to \(0\le \JensenShannonDivergence\le\ln 2\), which gives a fixed scale for comparing releases. For a sense of that scale, suppose the baseline sends 70 percent of its tool calls to tests and the rest to lint, and the candidate splits them evenly. That plainly visible shift gives \(\JensenShannonDivergence\) of about 0.021, roughly 3 percent of the 0.693 maximum that two configurations with no tool in common would reach. A useful alert threshold is therefore a small number, and it means something only against the divergence between two baseline samples of the same size, which shows how far the distribution moves by chance. The distributions must come from comparable inputs and one declared action-counting policy, and difference alone is not regression, since the intended repair may remove redundant calls. A shadow result can justify another test or a hold, whereas it cannot supply evidence for production effects that the shadow deliberately prevented.

14.5.2 Canary task assignment

Routing canary traffic per model call rather than per trajectory mixes baseline_v1 and candidate_v2 inside a single repair task, making any resulting failure or side effect impossible to attribute to either configuration. Because a canary is live execution with real effects, the release policy declares the eligible population, allocation rule, candidate configuration (model request, prompt, tools, harness, and verifier versions), comparison policy, resource limits, and stop conditions before any task is assigned. An allocation fraction \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) limits the share of eligible tasks assigned under that rule; it is neither a per-task authority grant nor a worst-case bound on total effects.

The policy assigns the configuration at the task or trajectory boundary and preserves it through continuation. Retries, resumed work, and delayed labels keep the original cohort identity, and dependent tasks from one customer or repository may need that larger unit of randomization. This is trajectory-level sticky routing, a stable configuration assignment rather than a promise to retain one physical model instance; workers can change through the resume protocol, and cohort assignment alone does not guarantee warm serving state.

↩︎ The resume protocol, under which a replacement worker takes ownership of a trajectory, is established in Resuming a Trajectory.

Outcome delay matters even when assignment is correct. Under the four-verdict rule of The four-verdict rule, every admitted task stays counted in its cohort, because counting only completed fast tasks can make a slow failing candidate look favorable. The analysis specifies when labels are mature and how unresolved cases affect promotion, and a failed label-delivery path is an evidence failure, not a reason to drop those tasks.

14.5.3 Sequential monitoring

Repeatedly applying a fixed-sample test after each new canary outcome inflates its false-positive rate well above the nominal level (Johari et al. 2017). A sequential design instead declares in advance how accumulating evidence will be used. The sequential probability ratio test (SPRT)6 (Wald 1945) is the compact example for two specified distributions, and it is a different procedure from the paired offline noninferiority test.

6 Sequential Probability Ratio Test: This is the sequential stopping rule of When to stop searching early applied to release rather than to search. Because sampling stops as soon as the evidence is decisive, the test typically reaches a decision with substantially fewer observations than a fixed-sample test with the same error targets, which for a canary means fewer candidate tasks exposed before the decision.

Let independent task labels \(\SequentialTestObservation_i\) be Bernoulli with a stationary acceptance probability, and let the simple hypotheses be \(\AcceptanceProbability=\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\) and \(\AcceptanceProbability=\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\), with \(0<\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1<\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0<1\) and the lower value a particular degradation. Both values are design inputs, not estimates. With \(\AcceptedLabelCount_n=\sum_{i=1}^{\ObservedLabelCount} \SequentialTestObservation_i\), the log-likelihood ratio favoring the degraded hypothesis is

\[\CumulativeLogLikelihoodRatio_n = \AcceptedLabelCount_n \ln\left(\frac{\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1}{\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0}\right) + (\ObservedLabelCount-\AcceptedLabelCount_n)\ln\left(\frac{1-\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1}{1-\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0}\right). \tag{2}\]

Success moves the statistic in equation 2 downward; failure moves it upward. Let \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel\) target the probability of choosing degradation under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\), and \(\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate\) the probability of choosing \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\) under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\). For \(0<\StatisticalSignificanceLevel,\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate<1\) and \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel+\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate<1\), Wald’s commonly used approximate log boundaries are

\[\SprtUpperLogBoundary = \ln\left(\frac{1-\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate}{\StatisticalSignificanceLevel}\right), \qquad \SprtLowerLogBoundary = \ln\left(\frac{\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate}{1-\StatisticalSignificanceLevel}\right). \tag{3}\]

Discrete observations can overshoot these approximate thresholds because a single task label jumps across the boundary in a finite step rather than stopping on it exactly. Calibrating the realized error probabilities therefore requires evaluating the discrete walk under the actual model (Fischer and Ramdas 2026).

Fischer, Lasse, and Aaditya Ramdas. 2026. “Improving Wald’s (Approximate) Sequential Probability Ratio Test by Avoiding Overshoot.” IEEE Transactions on Information Theory 72 (4): 2457–71. https://doi.org/10.1109/TIT.2026.3658855.

Evidence drifts toward hold under degradation and toward selecting \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\) otherwise; between the boundaries, keep sampling.

Systems Perspective 14.2: Reading a sequential decision
At \(\CumulativeLogLikelihoodRatio_n\ge \SprtUpperLogBoundary\), the test selects the specified degraded hypothesis and the release policy can stop new candidate admission. At \(\CumulativeLogLikelihoodRatio_n\le \SprtLowerLogBoundary\), it selects \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\) against that particular alternative. Between the boundaries, evidence remains inconclusive. Selecting \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\) rejects one specified degradation; general noninferiority, broader traffic, and safety each need their own evidence. A deadline or sample cap that ends collection between the boundaries leaves the decision inconclusive.

A production stream can violate this model through changing task mix, shared outages, correlated tasks, or delayed labels. Processing results in completion order creates selection bias when duration depends on outcome, which a declared admission-order analysis avoids at the cost of waiting behind unresolved tasks. Testing several metrics or restarting after an unfavorable path needs error control beyond these two boundaries.

The same simplified model gives a planning approximation for the expected stopping sample count. Under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\), each labeled task moves the log-likelihood ratio by an expected step of \(\SprtExpectedLogIncrement_1=\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\ln(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1/\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0)+(1-\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1)\ln((1-\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1)/(1-\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0))\). Neglecting boundary overshoot, the walk ends at the upper boundary \(\SprtUpperLogBoundary\) with probability \(1-\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate\) and at the lower boundary \(\SprtLowerLogBoundary\) with probability \(\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate\). Dividing that expected terminal position by the expected step per task gives the expected stopping sample count:

\[\mathbb{E}_1[\SprtStoppingSampleCount]\approx\frac{(1-\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate)\SprtUpperLogBoundary+\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate \SprtLowerLogBoundary}{\SprtExpectedLogIncrement_1}. \tag{4}\]

The numerator in equation 4 averages both terminal decisions, as in the classical average-sample-number approximation (Wald 1945; Hory and Hero 2004, eq. 24). Wald's sequential probability ratio test (SPRT) decision boundaries derives the approximate decision boundaries and stopping-sample expression while distinguishing target errors from realized errors.

Hory, Cyril, and Alfred O. Hero. 2004. Sequential Probability Ratio Test for the Detection of a Single Electron Spin in the OSCAR Setup. arXiv preprint quant-ph/0402181.

Napkin Math 14.3: Sequential evidence takes calendar time
Problem: Consider a separate, hypothetical later configuration that has met its offline and shadow prerequisites. Its monitor compares \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\) equal to 0.90 with \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\) equal to 0.75, using design targets \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel\) equal to 0.01 and \(\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate\) equal to 0.05. At 60 eligible tasks/hour and a 5 percent candidate allocation, what stopping-sample and calendar-time scale does the no-overshoot approximation predict under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\)?

Assumptions: Outcomes are independent with constant probability, admissions arrive at regular intervals, and each label arrives exactly 0.5 h after admission with none lost. The test is untruncated, with no operational tripwire.

Math: The approximate boundaries are 4.5539 and -2.9857. Under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\), the expected increment is 0.09233. Neglecting overshoot and substituting the target decision probabilities gives expected terminal log-likelihood 4.1769. Dividing that quantity by the expected increment gives approximately 45.24 labeled tasks. The average includes both possible decisions, including mistaken selection of \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0\); it is not the expected number until an alarm.

Candidate admissions occur at 3 tasks/hour. The corresponding admission-time scale is 15.08 h; adding the fixed label delay gives approximately 15.58 h until a decision. Tasks admitted while labels are pending can still be executing when the test stops.

Systems insight: The sample count is fixed by the hypotheses and error targets, so calendar time to a decision scales as \(1/\CanaryAllocationFraction\). Doubling the allocation to 10 percent cuts the time to about 8.0 h. Choose \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) from the decision deadline and from the exposure the release can tolerate to harms the monitor does not measure, and keep independent admission and effect limits, because an expected sample count is not a maximum.

The sample count and the canary fraction control different harms. Under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\), each candidate task fails about \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0-\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\) more often than it would under the baseline. With allocation fraction \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) of eligible arrivals at rate \(\ArrivalRate\) and label delay \(\CanaryLabelDelay\), the monitor decides after roughly \(\mathbb{E}_1[\SprtStoppingSampleCount]\) labeled tasks plus the \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\ArrivalRate \CanaryLabelDelay\) tasks admitted while their labels are pending, so the measured regression costs about \((\mathbb{E}_1[\SprtStoppingSampleCount]+\CanaryAllocationFraction\ArrivalRate \CanaryLabelDelay)(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0-\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1)\) excess failures. In the notebook’s scenario that is about 7 at the canary fraction and about 11 at full allocation. The fraction enters only through the label delay; its main effect on a measured regression is the calendar time needed to collect the samples.

Harms the monitor does not measure behave differently. They accrue for every admitted candidate task until some other mechanism detects them, so their rate scales with \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\), and this canary exposes candidate tasks at 5 percent of the full-allocation rate. The test’s sample size fixes the harm from a measured regression; the canary’s size limits the harms the test does not measure. A production release of a foundation model shows what the second kind of harm looks like when it escapes.

War Story 14.1: The GPT-4o sycophancy rollback (2025)
Context: In late April 2025, OpenAI rolled out an update to the GPT-4o model behind ChatGPT, adjusting its default personality (OpenAI 2025b).

Mechanism: By OpenAI’s account, the update focused too much on short-term user feedback and did not account for how users’ interactions evolve over time. The model skewed toward responses that were overly supportive but disingenuous. OpenAI’s follow-up analysis reports that changes including an added reward signal from user thumbs-up and thumbs-down feedback weakened the influence of the primary reward that had held sycophancy in check. The same analysis reports that the offline evaluations and the A/B test of the update both looked good before launch (OpenAI 2025a).

Impact: The update reached ChatGPT users before the behavior was recognized, and OpenAI rolled it back, returning users to an earlier version with more balanced behavior.

Fix: OpenAI committed to refining training techniques and system prompts to steer the model away from sycophancy, adding guardrails for honesty and transparency, expanding predeployment testing with direct user feedback, and expanding its evaluations to catch behavioral issues beyond sycophancy.

Systems lesson: A release gate passes exactly the criteria it declares. A regression in a property that no criterion measures clears every term of the gate, and no sequential monitor can stop it because none is watching it. The durable responses are a new criterion for the next release and an admission hold on the regressed configuration.

OpenAI. 2025a. Expanding on What We Missed with Sycophancy. OpenAI blog.

The sycophancy regression is that unmeasured harm at full scale. Detection came from outside the declared criteria, and the effective response was an admission stop on the regressed configuration followed by settlement of what it had already done.

14.5.4 Operational tripwires

Waiting for a sequential statistical monitor to accumulate dozens of labeled tasks after a candidate dispatches a forbidden tool call allows a deterministic policy breach to keep repeating until the sample threshold is met. A hard operational tripwire therefore does not wait for statistical evidence: a verified forbidden dispatch, an exhausted release allowance, or too much unresolved candidate work immediately stops further admission under the declared policy. A task-count cap bounds effects only when each task’s effects are also bounded at enforcing endpoints; a routing percentage alone supplies neither bound.

↩︎ The per-trajectory budget vector that caps each task’s turns, tokens, elapsed time, and cost is established in Turn, token, time, and dollar budgets.

Rollback changes which configuration may receive new work. It does not retract transmitted requests, restore changed objects, or make a baseline worker eligible to continue a candidate trajectory without the ownership and compatibility checks of Resuming a Trajectory. Outstanding candidate operations keep their original identities, and an explicit recovery policy governs their effects, cleanup, and residual owners. Detection, confirmed admission stop, and effect settlement are distinct intervals, and effects that cannot be undone or remain unknown at the cutoff stay out of any average that implies every case was restored.

↩︎ Settling residual effects through admitted compensation under an accountable owner, once model-directed work has stopped, is established in Residual settlement.

14.5.5 Release measurements

Reporting only the pass rate of completed canary tasks, or comparing the throughput of an arrival-limited 5 percent canary directly against a fully loaded baseline, can make a slow, stalling candidate look both accurate and fast. Release reporting therefore keeps a fraction of tasks, a rate of completion, and the resources spent producing them distinct. The accepted task is the unit that trajectory goodput (principle 15) charges all initiated work against. That principle is developed with the economics of agents at scale; the release record needs only its measured rate, defined below.

↪ Trajectory goodput as the throughput metric for sizing an agent platform is developed in Residence, service, and quota.

For a declared admitted cohort of size \(\InitiatedTasks\), let \(\AcceptedTasks\) count tasks meeting the complete acceptance contract by the observation cutoff, so \(\AcceptanceFraction=\AcceptedTasks/\InitiatedTasks\). Pending and unknown cases sit in \(\InitiatedTasks\) as not accepted under the default of the four-verdict rule, and the report lists them separately from established failures.

For an observation interval \([\ObservationIntervalStart,\ObservationIntervalEnd)\), let \(\AcceptedTasks[\ObservationIntervalStart,\ObservationIntervalEnd)\) count acceptance events in that interval under the declared measurement policy. Over that interval, trajectory goodput is measured as

\[\TrajectoryGoodput = \frac{\AcceptedTasks[\ObservationIntervalStart,\ObservationIntervalEnd)}{\ObservationIntervalLength}, \qquad \ObservationIntervalLength=\ObservationIntervalEnd-\ObservationIntervalStart.\]

The denominator is elapsed observation time, not the sum of overlapping task durations, and the report states startup and drain treatment, concurrency, and arrival policy. A small canary can be arrival-limited, so its measured goodput is not comparable with a fully loaded baseline’s capacity. Table 7 keeps companion measurements tied to their denominators.

The paired comparison, diagnostic experiment, and sequential monitor answer different questions and cannot substitute for one another. A coherent release record brings them together with their fixtures, limits, and residual obligations so that the resulting decision can be inspected.

14.6 The Release Decision

In a paired evaluation cohort, candidate_v2 accepted more tasks than baseline_v1, while an isolated diagnostic probe pointed toward an effective observation-interface repair. Taken on their own, these indicators seem encouraging, but neither outcome satisfies a sound engineering release gate. The two results also concern different configurations. The 150-pair cohort compared candidate_v2 as submitted, and the worked trial of pool_repair_01 that produced ws_r19 sits outside that cohort. The interface repair changes candidate_v2’s observation adapter, so the configuration it would produce is a revision that needs its own paired evaluation; the probe is evidence about the defect, not about the revision’s cohort performance. Evaluating an agentic system requires an accountable evidence trail. Before routing production traffic to a new candidate, engineers determine which claims have verified empirical support, which operational obligations remain unresolved, and what follow-up experiment the data actually justifies.

14.6.1 The decision record

When an individual trial verdict, a paired cohort comparison, and a diagnostic probe sit in separate reports without their provenance, scope, and operational boundaries attached, a positive result on the localized probe can easily be mistaken for missing regression checks or fleet-wide superiority evidence. A structured decision record prevents those disparate experimental results from being conflated by bringing the worked individual trial, the paired cohort comparison, and the diagnostic interface experiment together in one place (table 8). Rather than duplicating bulky workspace snapshots, the record links directly to immutable, content-addressed artifacts and explicitly logs when trace payloads, token charges, or downstream verdicts remain unavailable.

14.6.2 Evidence readiness

Promoting candidate_v2 on the current evidence would ship a configuration whose trial-level regression checks are unfinished, whose interface repair has been tested on only one saved prefix, and whose paired comparison interval still crosses the degradation threshold. The decision record leaves those three engineering obligations open, each at a different layer of the evaluation stack.

  1. Trial-level verification: The targeted repair in ws_r19 passed its immediate assertion, but its full regression suite and final resource accounting remain pending. Engineers cannot mark an individual trial successful while side effects and outstanding token bills remain unquantified.
  2. Component-level diagnosis: The observation-interface modification proved effective on continuations from one saved prefix of pool_repair_01, but success from a single prefix does not demonstrate generalized capability. Validating the interface repair requires testing across diverse environments and workloads.
  3. Cohort-level release gate: The paired comparison across the task cohort yielded a confidence interval that spans the noninferiority threshold. The next comparison needs a preregistered paired sample designed for the anticipated gain, discordance, tolerance, and power.

Inspecting a single execution trace in greater detail resolves none of these three obligations. A granular trace can explain why an agent failed a specific step, but it cannot substitute for missing regression suites, multi-fixture validation, or cohort-wide statistical significance. Collecting that missing evidence under an established protocol is also distinct from altering the evaluation question itself. When a candidate fails to clear a deployment threshold, engineers may feel tempted to prune difficult tasks, drop incomplete trials, expand token budgets mid-flight, or tune task selection criteria. While these adjustments reliably inflate raw acceptance rates, they distort the operational meaning of the benchmark. Such post hoc filtering measures an artificially sanitized workload rather than genuine system capability. When task specifications, resource constraints, or operational boundaries legitimately change, the engineering team must record a revised evaluation protocol and test the configuration against a fresh, independent sample.

14.6.3 The declared decision rule

Even if candidate_v2 ran faster or cheaper than baseline_v1, a release gate cannot let a gain in latency or cost override an inconclusive acceptance comparison where the lower limit of the paired interval drops below the permissible degradation threshold \(-\NoninferiorityTolerance\). That empirical evidence establishes neither noninferiority nor superiority: the interval does not rule out the specified unacceptable loss, and it does not establish that candidate_v2 is inferior either. An inconclusive observed interval also does not by itself determine the study’s planned power. The planning count in Paired outcome comparison is about 217 paired evaluations if the observed advantage persists, and even that count clears the gate with only roughly even odds. A powered next sample therefore uses the paired-variance planning approximation in Statistical Evaluation Rigor, with its gain, discordance, and tolerance fixed in advance.

In production environments, engineering teams guard against wishful thinking by encoding deployment policies into explicit, declared decision rules. A release gate is formulated as a logical conjunction of independent criteria, each anchored to concrete empirical evidence:

\[\ShipDecision=\IndicatorFunction\!\left[\PairedDifferenceLowerLimit>-\NoninferiorityTolerance\right]\wedge \IndicatorFunction\!\left[\CandidateTrajectoryGoodput\ge(1-\GoodputRelativeTolerance)\,\BaselineTrajectoryGoodput\right]\wedge \IndicatorFunction\!\left[\CandidateTailCompletionTime\le \CompletionDeadline\right]\wedge \IndicatorFunction\!\left[\CandidateViolationCount=0\right]. \tag{5}\]

Each predicate in equation 5 enforces a distinct operational requirement:

  • Paired noninferiority (\(\IndicatorFunction\!\left[\PairedDifferenceLowerLimit>-\NoninferiorityTolerance\right]\)): The lower bound of the confidence interval for the paired acceptance difference, \(\PairedDifferenceLowerLimit\), must exceed the allowed degradation margin \(-\NoninferiorityTolerance\). Clearing it supports, at the interval’s stated nominal confidence, the claim that the candidate does not degrade task completion by more than the tolerance.
  • Goodput preservation (\(\IndicatorFunction\!\left[\CandidateTrajectoryGoodput\ge(1-\GoodputRelativeTolerance)\,\BaselineTrajectoryGoodput\right]\)): Candidate trajectory goodput \(\CandidateTrajectoryGoodput\) must match baseline goodput \(\BaselineTrajectoryGoodput\) within a declared relative tolerance \(\GoodputRelativeTolerance\), evaluated across matched traffic profiles, workloads, and compute capacity.
  • Tail-latency constraint (\(\IndicatorFunction\!\left[\CandidateTailCompletionTime\le \CompletionDeadline\right]\)): The empirical 99th-percentile task completion time \(\CandidateTailCompletionTime\) across the evaluation cohort must not exceed the operational ceiling \(\CompletionDeadline\). This bound serves as a cohort-level service-level objective rather than a per-task watchdog timeout.
  • Zero-violation tripwire (\(\IndicatorFunction\!\left[\CandidateViolationCount=0\right]\)): The count of verified safety or contract violations \(\CandidateViolationCount\), such as unauthorized data modifications, privilege escalations, or uncontained tool executions, must equal zero. A single verified breach trips the gate immediately. Engineers must note, however, that observing zero violations within an evaluation cohort demonstrates only the absence of detected failures on that specific sample; it does not constitute a mathematical proof of absolute safety.

Certain operational metrics serve as diagnostic triggers rather than binary gates.
