For example, if a behavioral-drift metric such as the tool-distribution divergence in equation 1 crosses its declared alert threshold, the gate pipeline routes the candidate to manual engineering review rather than failing it outright. A significant change in tool usage frequency is not necessarily an anomaly; it often represents the exact behavioral modification intended by an interface or prompt redesign.

Because the release rule is a strict boolean conjunction, a failure in any single predicate yields \(\ShipDecision = 0\). Exceptional tail latency, lower token expenses, or an unblemished safety record cannot compensate for an inconclusive or degraded task acceptance rate. At the same time, the gate enforces only the predicates explicitly written into its formula. Any failure mode the conjunction does not monitor passes through it, as war story 14.1 showed.

For the worked candidate the first predicate already fails, so the gate returns zero without evaluating the goodput, latency, and violation terms, none of which the worked evidence measures for candidate_v2. The decision is to retain baseline_v1 in production and withhold live traffic from candidate_v2. The engineering team’s immediate next steps are to complete the pending regression runs for ws_r19, reconcile open token and tool expenditures, and pre-register an appropriately powered sample size before collecting further paired comparison data.

Automated gates evaluate these conditions deterministically, yet the underlying metrics reflect varying degrees of statistical uncertainty. A release evaluation report must therefore label each criterion with its precise operational status: satisfied, failed, or inconclusive under the declared evidence rule. Furthermore, individual 95 percent confidence intervals evaluated across separate operational metrics do not compound into an overall 95 percent system guarantee. When \(\TestFamilySize\) statistical predicates are each allowed probability \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel\) of a wrong verdict, the chance that at least one of them is wrong is bounded only by \(\TestFamilySize\StatisticalSignificanceLevel\), since the probability of any error is at most the sum of the individual error probabilities. It approaches that bound when the individual errors rarely coincide. That combined probability is the family-wise error rate, and the simplest control divides the allowed error among the tests, testing each predicate at \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel/\TestFamilySize\). Production evaluation protocols must document their family-wise error accounting, specifying how they control for multiple hypothesis tests and for repeated peeking across sequential evaluation checkpoints.

Checkpoint 14.4: Defend the evaluation decision

An agent evaluation pipeline exists to produce an auditable decision, recorded immutably, that identifies the exact configuration under test, establishes what the empirical evidence proves, authorizes the next operational step, and transparently logs every duty that remains open. Skipping any link in that chain turns an operational measurement into a misleading release signal.

14.7 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Even an apparently thorough operational record can answer the wrong engineering question. The failure modes below break the link between operational evidence and deployment readiness, and each exacts a measurable cost in operational stability, exposure, or diagnostic visibility.

Pitfall: Relying on uniform head sampling to keep examples of rare failures.

Head sampling makes a retention decision at request ingestion, long before the agent’s final execution outcome is known (section 14.3). Because severe regressions and edge-case agent failures are inherently rare, uniform head sampling discards the vast majority of critical failure traces. At a 10 percent sampling rate, an intermittent failure mode that occurs 5 times across an evaluation run leaves no detailed trace 59 percent of the time, blinding engineers during root-cause debugging. Systems should instead employ tail sampling keyed to task outcomes and anomalous step traces. Engineers should retain a small, fixed uniform control sample to validate aggregate rates, while computing production failure rates from complete, lightweight accounting telemetry rather than sampled execution traces.

Fallacy: A small canary fraction limits the damage a regression can do.

For regressions that automated monitors track, the total blast radius depends on the sample count required to achieve statistical confidence rather than the traffic split percentage (section 14.5). In the reference monitor, a decision needs the same expected number of labeled tasks at any allocation (notebook 14.3), so the measured regression costs about 7 excess failures at a 5 percent allocation and about 11 at full allocation. A small fraction trims only the failures among tasks admitted while labels are pending, and it stretches the time to a decision to 15.58 h. A small traffic fraction limits damage only for unmonitored hazards or qualitative failures that human operators spot manually. Automated deployment systems must therefore enforce hard circuit-breaker limits and absolute failure thresholds that operate independently of the canary traffic fraction.

Pitfall: Treating a clean diagnostic run or a rollback as closing the incident.

A successful diagnostic run demonstrates stability only within the narrow confines of its specific test fixture (section 14.4.3). Statistically, observing 20 accepted runs out of 20 diagnostic executions still leaves the true failure rate as high as 16 percent at 95 percent confidence. Furthermore, rolling back candidate code or throttling traffic merely prevents new failures; it cannot retract state mutations, external tool invocations, or corrupted database records that the agent already generated. Teams must maintain regression monitoring, enforce bounded exposure limits, and retain designated recovery owners until all asynchronous downstream effects have fully settled.

Pitfall: Reading a successful span or a zero exit code as a passed task.

Span status records how one operation ended, not whether the task met its contract (section 14.2.2). A test process can exit cleanly while its output shows failed assertions, and a client span can end in a timeout while the candidate still produces a valid patch from cached context. A dashboard that counts green spans as accepted work therefore reports activity rather than acceptance, and the gap widens exactly when verdicts arrive late, after the execution spans have closed. Traces should carry a reference to the task verdict rather than a copy of it, and every acceptance figure should come from the accounting record, where pending and unknown verdicts stay in the denominator.

Fallacy: A configuration that clears every term of the release gate is safe to release.

A conjunctive gate enforces exactly the predicates written into it and nothing else (section 14.6.3). A regression in a property no criterion measures clears every term, and no sequential monitor stops it, because none is watching that property. The sycophancy rollback of war story 14.1 followed offline evaluations and an A/B test that both looked good. Zero observed violations in a cohort also bound the violation rate only as tightly as the cohort’s size allows. A passed gate supports the scoped claim its predicates state, and the canary’s allocation, the hard tripwires, and a recovery owner for every outstanding effect remain the protection against what the gate does not measure.

14.8 Summary

An agent configuration that acts on live work produces evidence that no offline comparison collects, and operating it well means deciding in advance which of that evidence to keep and what each piece can establish. A trace binds model calls, tool executions, and checks to their trial, but a span’s status is not a task verdict, and summing overlapping spans overstates elapsed time that only the critical path explains. Retention keeps rare failures only when selection waits for verdicts, while the accounting record, which is never sampled, supplies every population rate. A postmortem separates the findings each boundary supports and tests an explanation by intervening on a saved prefix, so its conclusions hold for that fixture and need broader evaluation before they support a release. Staged exposure then trades calendar time against harms the monitor does not measure, and a rollback stops new admission without settling what the candidate already did. The worked case ends where the evidence does. The interface repair is supported on its fixture, the offline comparison remains inconclusive, and the declared gate returns zero, so baseline_v1 stays in production while the team collects the evidence the decision record names as missing.

The quantitative decisions developed across this chapter rely on the mathematical relationships summarized in table 9. Each formulation either sizes the operational evidence required to support a claim or translates that evidence into a scoped promotion decision.

Key Takeaways: Keep operational evidence scoped to what it shows
  • A trace correlates; it does not authorize or accept: Spans bind model calls, tool executions, and checks to their trial and artifact, and the critical path through their dependencies, not the sum of their durations, explains elapsed time. A diagnostic trace may be sampled or truncated, so it never replaces the durable recovery log or the task verdict.
  • Retention decides what diagnosis can see: Uniform head sampling before a run completes systematically misses rare failure modes. Conversely, verdict-aware retention prioritizes intermittent bugs but skews raw telemetry distributions. Population failure rates come from complete accounting records; estimates from diagnostic samples require appropriate inclusion probabilities and reweighting.
  • A postmortem tests an explanation by intervening: Findings stay separated by boundary (interface defect, model response, runtime refusal), and a hypothesis is tested by a controlled branch from a saved prefix, not by rereading telemetry. Its conclusion holds for that fixture; broader claims need the evaluation of a revised configuration.
  • Canary allocation and monitoring control different harms: Sequential hypothesis testing, such as Wald’s SPRT, sets the expected sample count needed to reach a decision on a measured regression. Reducing the canary allocation primarily extends the time needed to collect that evidence while lowering the exposure rate to unmonitored harms. Unmonitored failure modes bypass statistical canary monitors entirely and require hard operational tripwires. Furthermore, rolling back routing terminates only incoming traffic; repairing external mutations and state corruption requires explicit recovery mechanisms.
  • A release is a decision record, not a scorecard: A production release gate mandates satisfying every declared acceptance criterion simultaneously. Superior performance on task accuracy cannot compensate for regressions in tail latency, budget breaches, or safety violations. Inconclusive trials must never be converted into default approvals, and rigorous experimental protocols require fixing hypotheses, sample sizes, and decision thresholds before collecting evaluation data.

Enforcing causal and statistical release evidence (principle 12) requires a release protocol to specify its task criteria, operational boundaries, exposure policy, and statistical uncertainty explicitly. By tying release gates directly to structured execution traces and paired counterfactual baselines, systems engineers ensure that promotion decisions rest on reproducible evidence with stated limits rather than on summary scorecards.

14.9 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The statistics of staged release are older than electronic computing. During World War II, Abraham Wald developed the sequential probability ratio test (SPRT) to optimize industrial quality inspection, showing that deciding acceptance or rejection dynamically as evidence accumulates minimizes the expected sample size relative to fixed-horizon trials (Wald 1945).

Lamport, Leslie. 1978. “Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System.” Communications of the ACM 21 (7): 558–65. https://doi.org/10.1145/359545.359563.
Fonseca, Rodrigo, George Porter, Randy H. Katz, Scott Shenker, and Ion Stoica. 2007. “X-Trace: A Pervasive Network Tracing Framework.” 4th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI), 271–84.
Kohavi, Ron, Roger Longbotham, Dan Sommerfield, and Randal M. Henne. 2009. “Controlled Experiments on the Web: Survey and Practical Guide.” Data Mining and Knowledge Discovery 18 (1): 140–81. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10618-008-0114-1.
Johari, Ramesh, Pete Koomen, Leonid Pekelis, and David Walsh. 2017. “Peeking at A/B Tests: Why It Matters, and What to Do about It.” Proceedings of the 23rd ACM SIGKDD International Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining, 1517–25. https://doi.org/10.1145/3097983.3097992.
OpenAI. 2025b. Sycophancy in GPT-4o: What Happened and What We’re Doing about It. OpenAI blog.

The telemetry required to diagnose multi-turn agent execution draws directly from distributed systems observability. Lamport established that asynchronous events across decoupled components admit only a partial order unless system metadata explicitly captures their causal links (Lamport 1978). Fonseca et al. (2007) extended this principle to modern network architectures with X-Trace, propagating deterministic task contexts across diverse protocol layers and administrative domains to reconstruct complete end-to-end execution paths. In the same years, production web services transformed deployment validation into continuous experimentation: Kohavi et al. (2009) codified online controlled experiments with staged canary deployments as standard release gates. Sigelman et al. (2010) then proved in Dapper that distributed tracing could operate continuously at production scale by decoupling span collection from the critical path and employing aggressive head-based sampling to bound runtime overhead to negligible levels. Google’s site reliability engineering practice established the blameless postmortem, institutionalizing structured incident postmortems as essential operational records for diagnosing systemic failures (Beyer et al. 2016). When engineering teams began monitoring live experimentation dashboards in real time, Johari et al. (2017) proved that repeated significance testing inflates false-positive rates far above the nominal level, establishing why sequential testing bounds, the descendants of Wald’s test, must govern automated canary rollouts. Agent releases add what Wald’s inspection lines did not face: labels that arrive hours after admission, tasks whose effects outlive the decision, and harms that no monitored metric records, the gap that the 2025 sycophancy rollback exposed (OpenAI 2025b).

Further reading.

  • Wald, “Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses” (Wald 1945): The original derivation of the sequential probability ratio test, its approximate decision boundaries, and its average sample number, providing the mathematical foundation for staged canary deployments and early-stopping test monitors.
  • Sigelman et al., “Dapper” (Sigelman et al. 2010): The foundational architecture of production distributed tracing under strict overhead budgets, examining the sampling policies and trace contexts that enable fine-grained post-hoc diagnosis.
  • Kohavi, Tang, and Xu, Trustworthy Online Controlled Experiments (Kohavi et al. 2020): A practitioner’s guide to randomized experimentation in production systems, covering assignment units, sample-ratio mismatches, and the statistical pitfalls that invalidate live rollout decisions.
  • Beyer et al., Site Reliability Engineering (Beyer et al. 2016): The practitioner’s account of operating production services, including the blameless postmortem and the release engineering that agent operations extend from people to components.
Wald, Abraham. 1945. “Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses.” The Annals of Mathematical Statistics 16 (2): 117–86. https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177731118.
Kohavi, Ron, Diane Tang, and Ya Xu. 2020. Trustworthy Online Controlled Experiments: A Practical Guide to A/B Testing. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108653985.

14.10 Exercises

Each problem states its constraints and ends in a decision about what the evidence supports or what to collect next. Givens are hypothetical unless stated otherwise, and every interval is a nominal 95 percent interval with \(\IntervalNormalQuantile=1.96\).

1. Where the time went (Basic). A repair-agent timing trial records these spans on one host clock, in seconds after segment start. Retrieval runs 0.0 to 1.2, a model call proposing checks 1.2 to 4.0, targeted tests 4.0 to 13.0, a static lint check 4.0 to 7.5, a dependency audit 4.0 to 10.0, and a model call synthesizing the report 14.0 to 17.0. The three checks are independent of one another, synthesis waits for all three, and the enclosing segment ends at 17.5. (a) Compute the sum of the leaf span durations and the critical-path duration, and find how much of the 17.5 s segment the spans leave unattributed. (b) A cache cuts the targeted tests to 5.0 s with everything else unchanged. Compute the new critical path and the saving, and name the span that now limits the check phase. (c) A routing change instead halves both model calls. Compute its saving, compare it with part (b), and state which assumption about the unattributed intervals both estimates share. (d) Decide which change to fund first if each costs the same to build, and name the controlled measurement that must confirm the saving before it is claimed.

2. A retention budget for rare failures (Intermediate). A repair fleet runs 20,000 evaluation trials per day, each with a 2 MB (decimal) permitted trace body. At the selection boundary, 3 percent of trials have failed a criterion, 1 percent remain pending or unknown, and the rest are ordinary accepted trials. One failure mode occurs 8 times per week, and downstream diagnostic storage is capped at 1.5 GB per day. (a) Compute the daily volume of full retention and of uniform head sampling at \(\HeadSamplingProbability=2\) percent, and the probability that head sampling keeps no detailed trace of the rare mode in a week. (b) Compute the daily volume of tail selection that keeps every failed and unresolved trial plus a 1 percent random control sample of ordinary trials, and check it against the cap. (c) Design a selection policy within the cap that keeps every failed trial. State the sampling rates for unresolved and ordinary trials, the inclusion probabilities the record must keep, and one change to the trace body that would relax the cap. (d) An analyst reports the fraction of retained traces under the policy of part (b) that failed as the fleet’s failure rate. Compute the reported value, and name the record the failure rate must come from.

3. Drift in shadow execution (Intermediate). A shadow run of the revised repair configuration records its tool calls over four categories, read, test, lint, and edit. The baseline distributes its calls as 0.40, 0.30, 0.10, and 0.20, and the candidate as 0.30, 0.45, 0.05, and 0.20. Two independent baseline samples of the same size differ by a Jensen–Shannon divergence of 0.004 nats. (a) Compute \(\JensenShannonDivergence\) between baseline and candidate with equation 1, in nats, and express it as a fraction of its maximum, \(\ln 2\). (b) Release policy routes a candidate to engineering review when its divergence exceeds three times the baseline-versus-baseline value. Decide whether this candidate is routed, and whether the decision would change under a five-times rule. (c) The candidate is the paginated observation interface, which retrieves remaining results instead of guessing. Explain why more test calls and fewer reads may be the intended change, and state which measurement, not the divergence, decides whether the shift is an improvement. (d) The shadow copies also read from a production ticketing service. Name three effects such reads can have and the declarations the shadow design must make before it mirrors any input.

4. Postmortem of a wrong refund answer (Advanced). The support agent tells a customer that a purchase is refundable within 60 days, while the current policy allows 30. The retained trace holds three records. The raw retrieval result contains two policy passages, the current version and a superseded one, each with an effective date; the delivered context contains both passages with the effective dates removed by the observation adapter; and the answer quotes the superseded passage. The team restores a compatible saved prefix ending just before the retrieval call and runs 40 fresh continuations in each of three branches, holding the model configuration, sampling settings, prompt, and a policy-grounded reference check fixed; every run reaches a known verdict. Branch A keeps the original adapter and yields 18 wrong answers. Branch B delivers the effective dates and yields 3 wrong answers. Branch C removes the superseded passage from the index, so only the current version is retrieved, and yields none. Release policy requires the Wilson upper limit on the support agent’s per-session wrong-answer rate to be at most 2 percent. (a) For each claim, state whether the retained record alone, a regression replay of the captured result without a model call, or only the intervention can establish it: the adapter removes effective dates; the model received both passages; the missing dates contributed to the wrong answer. (b) Compute each branch’s wrong-answer rate with its Wilson interval. Decide whether the data support the claim that delivering effective dates reduces wrong answers from this prefix, and whether 40 runs per branch distinguish branch B from branch C. (c) Compute how many consecutive correct continuations a fix would need before its Wilson upper limit met the release policy, and the corresponding lower confidence bound on five-session consistency from branch C’s upper limit at 40 runs, assuming independent recurrences from this same saved prefix. (d) A teammate reports a fourth branch that delivers the dates and also lowers the sampling temperature to zero, with 40 of 40 correct, and proposes shipping it as the fix. Critique the proposal, choose a corrective change, and write the postmortem record’s lines for established findings, tested change, and remaining questions.

5. Size a canary against a deadline (Advanced). A repair-agent canary monitors acceptance with the SPRT of equation 2, comparing \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0=0.80\) with \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1=0.70\) at design targets \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel=0.05\) and \(\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate=0.10\). Eligible tasks arrive regularly at 40 per hour, each label arrives 2 hours after admission when the regression suite finishes, and under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\) the planning target is an approximate expected decision time of at most 24 hours from the first admission. Separately, a harm the monitor does not measure occurs in 0.5 percent of candidate tasks, and release policy requires the candidate to run for 7 days at its allocation before any wider release. (a) Compute \(\SprtUpperLogBoundary\), \(\SprtLowerLogBoundary\), \(\SprtExpectedLogIncrement_1\), and the no-overshoot \(\mathbb{E}_1[\SprtStoppingSampleCount]\). (b) Find the smallest allocation \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) that meets that expected-time target. (c) Compute the expected excess failures from the measured regression at that \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) and at \(\CanaryAllocationFraction=25\) percent. (d) If the allocation continues for all seven days, compute the expected count of unmeasured harms over the 7-day period at both allocations. Choose \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) and justify it from parts (b) through (d). Explain why this exposure calculation is not an instruction to keep admitting the candidate after a degradation alarm. (e) A teammate proposes replacing the SPRT with a fixed-sample test rerun after every new label, stopping at the first value below 0.05. Critique the proposal.

6. Critique a release report (Advanced). A team asks to promote a repair-agent candidate with the following evidence. The candidate accepted 78 percent of tasks and the baseline 72 percent, reported as a 6-point gain. The baseline ran on 100 tasks sampled last month, and the candidate on 100 newly sampled tasks. The team dropped 12 candidate trials whose regression suites had not finished, so the candidate accepted 69 of the remaining 88. Report quality counted as accepted when an LLM judge scored it at least 4 of 5, justified by 90 percent raw agreement with a second judge. The failure rate in the release notes was computed from tail-sampled traces. (a) Under the four-verdict rule, bound the candidate’s accepted fraction of its admitted cohort, and state what the 6-point claim becomes. (b) Using equation 13.3 with \(\PooledAcceptanceProbability=0.75\), a 5 percent two-sided significance target, and 80 percent power, compute the tasks per independent arm needed to detect a 6-point difference. (c) Redesign the comparison as paired. Assuming a discordant fraction \(\DiscordantPairFraction=0.15\), an anticipated gain of 3 points, and \(\NoninferiorityTolerance=2\) points, find the number of task pairs at which the normal-approximation lower limit \(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference-\IntervalNormalQuantile\sqrt{(\DiscordantPairFraction-\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference^2)/\EvaluationSampleCount}\) clears \(-\NoninferiorityTolerance\) if the gain holds. Contrast this expected-limit-centering count with the fixed-variance normal planning count at 80 percent power, using power quantile 0.84. Both calculations assume the stated gain and discordance; the powered count approximates a normal gate rather than calibrating the exact paired-score interval. (d) Identify the remaining defects in the report and write a defensible protocol: which criteria the automated gate enforces, which measurements the judge must carry, and which conditions the team must declare before collecting data.

What’s Next: From operational evidence to curated trajectories
Operation leaves a qualified record: verdicts held in the accounting record, traces retained with their inclusion probabilities, and a postmortem that separated an interface defect from a model behavior and a runtime refusal. Trajectory Curation uses that record to guide diagnosis, admission, and corpus design, and its first decision is triage. The truncated-observation failure that the postmortem traced largely to the observation adapter belongs in an interface fix, not in a corpus. A logged success is not automatically a useful demonstration, and a failure is not automatically a policy defect. Preserve criterion and configuration provenance, unresolved evidence, and the separation between development data and held-out evaluation. Curation turns that qualified record into a learning-data decision.
